@@64041083 <p> THE Irish economy have been scarred by Brexit , the pandemic and now the horrific war in Ukraine waged by a callous tyrant , but climate change remains the overarching existential problem facing the entire planet and every living being on it . The war has fundamentally changed EU policy and the global context in which it will be applied , most notably the energy transition and sustainable food production . To achieve a smooth transition , Ireland will need to radically transform its energy system : reducing energy demand and moving away from fossil fuels to zero or low-carbon fuels and power sources . The agricultural sector will of course have a big role to play in reaching climate neutrality , so let 's start giving them some carrots and not just the stick . Biogas production has been highlighted as a key way to help fortify the EU 's struggling farm sector against increasing energy costs . Anaerobic digestion is the controlled use of biodegradable organic materials to produce renewable energy in the form of biogas and organic fertiliser . With squeezed incomes for farmers , the use of biogas can provide an opportunity for additional @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ clean , renewable , and reliable source of energy as well as a new source of income for farmers . Limerick is full of fertile agricultural land , a fully-fledged system AD facilities would have numerous benefits for the agricultural sector , but also rural economies more broadly . @@64041267 <h> ' Disappointing ' increases in Ireland 's greenhouse gas emissions <p> Older plants such as the coal-fired plant at Moneypoint were used to ensure power was available last year , contributing to the rise in carbon intensity of electricity production . File picture : Kieran Clancy <p> Ireland 's greenhouse gas emissions from energy generation and industry shot up last year , with emissions from electricity generation alone rising 21% , an increase branded as " disappointing " by the Environmental Protection Agency ( EPA ) . <p> In its annual review , the EPA found greenhouse gas emissions from Irish energy generation and industrial production rose by 15% , or 2m tonnes , last year , compared to 2020 . <p> The EPA said this rise compares unfavourably with other European countries as the EU Commission found an increase of about 9.1% across the region . <p> The increase is largely linked to the greater carbon intensity of electricity production last year , according to the EPA : <p> Several factors came together to compound this -- a decrease in wind-power availability , some relatively modern gas-fired plants being offline , and an increase in electricity demand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at Moneypoint , were used to ensure that power was available . " <p> Aviation emissions also increased , although the review notes the new total of 5.3m tonnes is still much lower than the pre-pandemic level of 12.8m tonnes . <p> EPA director general Laura Burke said that , between 2017 and 2019 , the emissions trading system sector saw a fall in emissions largely driven by the higher use of renewable energy sources on the National Grid here . Covid lockdowns led to continued decreases in 2020 , she said . <p> " The increase we are seeing for 2021 sector is disappointing and is a reminder of the need for policies and decisions which support sustained emissions reductions in power generation and industrial emissions , " said Ms Burke . <p> The challenges faced in the power-generation sector are in sharp focus at the moment . We must ensure that we are taking decisions now which recognise the urgency of the climate change challenge and that will deliver reductions in emissions . <p> The EPA found that industrial emissions increased by about 7% , with cement industry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lunchtime News Wrap <p> Sign up to to get the latest news direct to your inbox daily at 1pm <p> Puzzles hub <p> Visit our brain gym where you will find simple and cryptic crosswords , sudoku puzzles and much more . @@64042469 <h> EU to develop a digital twin of Earth to better predict climate impact <p> The project is being supported with an initial ? 150m investment , with the goal to create a ' full ' digital replica of the Earth by 2030 . <p> The European Commission has launched a new initiative to help tackle the climate emergency by developing a highly accurate digital model of Earth . <p> The Destination Earth initiative aims to help monitor , model and predict natural and human activity , and develop and test scenarios for more sustainable development . <p> The project will be supported with an initial ? 150m from the Digital Europe programme until mid-2024 . The information , scenarios , forecasts and visualisations will be provided first to public sector users , before gradually going to scientific communities , the private sector and the general public . <p> " With Destination Earth , we are building on Europe 's strong cards , " EU commissioner for the internal market Thierry Breton said . <p> It is hoped the prediction capabilities of this model will help protect biodiversity , manage water , monitor renewable energy and mitigate disaster risks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ developed with the help of the European Space Agency , the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts and the European Organisation for the Exploitation of Meteorological Satellites . <p> The EU plans to develop the project gradually , with a few key planned milestones . By 2024 , it is hoped the system will be composed of a core system , a data lake and digital twins . <p> The core system will provide decision-making tools , applications and services , based on a secure cloud-computing system . The data lake will provide storage space and access to data and will be built upon existing scientific datasets . <p> The digital twins will combine data from real-time observations and simulations . One will focus on weather-induced and geophysical hazards such as floods and earthquakes , while the other will focus on supporting mitigation scenarios for the climate emergency . <p> The project then aims to create a ' full ' digital replica of the Earth by 2030 . <p> " This initiative is a clear example that we can not fight climate change without digital technologies , " executive VP @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Vestager , said . " For example , the digital modelling of the earth will help to predict major environmental degradation with unprecedented reliability . " <p> Climate is a key focus area for the EU with the European Green Deal . Last month , EU finance commissioner Mairead McGuinness told Irish businesses that private investment is key to achieving climate goals in Ireland and the EU by 2050 . <p> 10 things you need to know direct to your inbox every weekday . @@64043778 <p> The new proposals for the upgrade to the road between Cork and Limerick include the notion of promoting " active travel " - cycling and walking . Nudging people towards options which do n't involve the use of fossil fuels is no longer just politically correct . <p> In an era of climate change , it is imperative . We have to be prompted to take wider considerations such as the good of the environment into the decision-making process . <p> Irish people are fond of tax incentives , and tax incentives of various types have been used over many years to change our decisions about travel and especially how we choose to commute . <p> The annual motor tax and the system of vehicle registration tax are regularly adjusted to favour less polluting vehicles . <p> Even the ban within the tax system to " salary sacrifice " , the practice of substituting cash wages for some other less readily taxable benefit , is suspended when it comes to options for commuting . <p> Under the Cycle to Work scheme , employers are allowed to provide bicycles and e-bikes to their employees paid from the gross wages @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ savings for some employees . <p> Travel vouchers <p> Employees are also permitted to use travel vouchers paid out of their gross salary under the Tax Saver Commuter ticket scheme , again with tax savings . <p> It is not clear how effective these incentives have been in changing commuting behaviour in the past . <p> A detailed study carried out by the Department of Transport late last year on the Cycle to Work scheme examined the shift in commuting habits as reported in census figures . The report concluded that the increase in commuting by bicycle was not in direct proportion to the number of claimants under the Cycle to Work scheme . <p> However , the report found that the estimated exchequer cost of running the scheme at some ? 4m per annum is tiny relative to the overall tax take . It 's a socially useful benefit at a modest cost . <p> The impact of the Tax Saver commuter tickets is even harder to judge given the distortions of the pandemic . <p> Many workers no longer had to commute to their office jobs , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ benefit . <p> As people now return to their workplaces perhaps for only a few days a week , should there be cheaper , more flexible , and tax efficient commuter tickets available ? <p> I gather that the Department of Transport is examining the situation , and for once the problem does not lie with inflexibility in the tax system . The Tax Acts do n't insist that travel vouchers have to be for everyday use to be effective in reducing the person 's tax bill . Instead , the issue seems to lie with the logistics of developing readily usable and enforceable two-day or three-day weekly passes . <p> It is understandable if there is some official reluctance to commit significant time and resources to developing a solution for what may only be a temporary phase of partial return to offices and other workplaces . There is also an argument that if commuting is only required one or two days a week , it might not be an efficient use of public funds to subsidise these relatively modest costs , particularly as a temporary 20% reduction in all @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ inflationary pinch . <p> However it would be unwise to overlook any opportunity to reinforce the importance of the public transport system in addressing climate change . An efficient and tax subsidised flexible commuter ticket could be very effective in doing that . <p> Brian Keegan is director of public policy at Chartered Accountants Ireland 
@@64043984 <h> Cork " in the zone " with clean air initiative <p> CORK 'S Lord Mayor is waging war against climate change as he prepares to introduce the first designated clean air zone of its kind in an Irish city . <p> Cork 's Clean Air Zone will see areas including Oliver Plunkett Street , St Patrick 's Street , Grand Parade and South Mall fitted with air monitors by the start of June . <p> These will measure nitrogen oxides ( NOx ) , ozone and particulate matter over time . NOx is a specific measure of pollution attributable to petrol or diesel engines . Cork City Council has partnered with Cork Healthy Cities , the Centre for Research into Atmospheric Chemistry , UCC 's School of Applied Psychology , Cork Chamber of Commerce and Cork Business Association to make the initiative possible . The CAZ has been described as the start of a long partnership that will see the development of a play street in the area and school-based initiatives aimed at raising awareness . <p> Lord Mayor , Cllr Colm Kelleher described the importance of the measures . <p> ' ' This Clean Air Zone is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ city , " he said . " Our vision for Cork 's Clean Air Zone is that it will improve the urban environment to support public health and the local economy , making Cork a more attractive place to live , work , and spend leisure time ' ' . <p> The rollout of the Clean Air Zone is led by Executive Scientist with Cork City Council , Dr Kevin Ryan . <p> He explained that a Clean Air Zone is an area where targeted action is taken to improve air quality , specifically by reducing major sources of pollution . <p> " Clean Air Zones address numerous types of pollution , including nitrogen dioxide and particulate matter , " he said . " Tailored measures to improve air quality will reduce public exposure to these harmful pollutants " he said . <p> Cork City Council commissioned Cork-based artist , Kevin O'Brien to create an artwork depicting different aspects of air quality in the city to mark the event . <p> " Cork 's Clean Air Zone is a major milestone for the city " , he said . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a major source of particles and nitrogen oxides . Pedestrianising this area will result in cleaner air and less traffic noise " .

@@64044623 <h> The Leaving Certificate is getting a long-overdue shake up , but will the reform reduce stress ? <h> With new subjects being added , a greater weight being put on continuous assessment and some exams being sat in fifth year , the senior cycle is getting a much needed overhaul . <p> Minister for Education Norma Foley recently announced an ambitious plan to shake up the traditional Leaving Cert set up following an advisory report from the National Council for Curriculum and Assessment ( NCCA ) that 's been four years in the making . A report that takes into consideration the voice of students , learnings of educators and international experience , the new plan of action promises to make sure that the student is at the centre of their senior cycle experience . <p> The three core pillars of this reimagining are the empowerment of students to meet the challenges of the modern world , the enrichment of the student experience and the embedding of wellbeing and stress reduction . Putting an emphasis on additional assessment components , spreading out project work across fifth and sixth year , and revising the curricula for all existing subjects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from September 2023 . <h> New changes <p> Under the new regime , 40% of final grades will come from assessment components such as project work , orals or practicals , with a final written exam making up the remaining 60% . The first papers of both English and Irish will be sat at the end of fifth year , and oral exams and music practicals will take place during the first week of the Easter holidays in sixth year . <p> The NCCA will invite a varied group of network schools to take part in an early implementation of the changes to provide feedback , and will publish a schedule of dates for when individual subject specifications will be completed and delivered by September 2023 . New subjects and revised curricula for certain subjects will be rolled out to fifth year students in September 2024 . <h> New subjects <p> There are not one , but two new subjects being added to the Leaving Cert curriculum -- Drama , Film and Theatre Studies , and Climate Action and Sustainable Development -- in order to increase choice , match interests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ state of climate emergency , studies into climate change and sustainability will allow students to get to grips with these global challenges that will have an impact on their future . <p> Likewise , Drama , Film and Theatre Studies is a welcome addition to the senior cycle . Recognising arts as a crucial aspect of our culture , and elevating these areas of merit will allow young people to foster and harness their interests . Pursuing a career in the arts has long been discouraged in favour of a financially stable path , and this is a step towards unpicking that mindset . <h> An endurance test <p> How many of us still have nightmares set in the silence of an exam hall on a sunny day in June ? An exam that seems to carry so much weight -- ' you 'll never sit anything as tough again ' -- the Leaving Cert is a source of extreme pressure for students . Reform within the curriculum is necessary , but further revisions of the points system and third-level progression routes need to be considered before students will be able to breathe a true sigh of relief .

@@64044796 <p> When Irish microbiologist Davida Smyth signed up for Twitter , she may never have expected to be an ardent follower of a " Covid poops " account . <p> The global dashboard @COVIDPoops19 is a platform for " all things wastewater " and Sars-CoV-2 , and has proved to be an invaluable communication tool for scientists such as her . <p> The handle has retweeted some of Smyth 's own research with colleagues on mysterious new strains of coronavirus in the New York public sewage system . <p> Smyth , who is an associate professor of molecular microbiology at the Texas A&amp;M University-San Antonio , is joint author of a paper recently published in the scientific journal Nature Communications on the results of that research . <p> As she explains , testing wastewater has always been an invaluable and economically efficient way of tracing and tracking harmful bacteria , such as E.coli . <p> About five months after Covid-19 hit the US , Smyth collaborated with a group of scientists at the City University of New York who were commissioned to test wastewater in conjunction with the city 's department of environmental protection . <p> The work with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out for differences that would indicate an increase in cases and the possible evolution of new variants . <p> This involved using tiny filters to separate out the virus from the water , and converting the coronavirus spike RNA to DNA , where it was then isolated and sequenced . <p> When the Omicron variant was reported in South Africa , the team began looking for it in the wastewater systems and detected it two weeks before it was confirmed in Covid patients in New York . <p> Last January they also began to observe something unusual -- four variants or " cryptic lineages " that had not so far been detected by clinical health teams in New York . <p> So where were these new variants coming from ? This was the million-dollar question , Smyth says . <p> Some of the scientists suggested it could be linked to animals with the virus , from dogs and cats to squirrels and rats . <p> Others , such as Smyth , suspect the variants may be linked to people whose infections are not being tracked by established detection systems . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these new variants were coming from a handful of the plants , " she explains . <p> " We were aware that Omicron was detected first in South Africa , where there is a high incidence of HIV-Aids . <p> It could be that these new variants are linked to specific immune-compromised patients treated in one or two clinical settings , or it could even be that these variants are being created and shed by one single person . <p> The research highlights the value of studying wastewater systems as a cheaper and faster way of keeping on top of the virus , she says . <p> " Instead of testing individuals for variants , a wastewater sample covers thousands , " she explains . <p> The information allows local authorities to act quickly when a new strain emerges , allowing for specific quarantining , Smyth explains . <p> " Ireland is doing well at this and it 's important because Covid-19 is not going away any time soon , " she says . <p> Ireland has over 1,100 public wastewater catchment areas . @@64045244 <h> Ending the age of fossil fuels ? Answers to key questions on UN climate report <p> Flames from the Dixie wildfire in California during the summer of 2021 . Photo : Getty Images <p> Share this article <p> 04/04/2022 20:16 PM <p> By Emily Beament , PA Environment Correspondent <p> The latest climate report from the United Nations ( UN ) sets out the action needed to tackle the global warming crisis . <p> Here are answers to some key questions about the report - and what you need to know about its findings . <h> What is the report ? <p> It is the third part of a global assessment of climate science by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) , the sixth such assessment the UN body has conducted , with the most recent one back in 2013/14 . <p> This third report looks at " mitigation " or the action needed to curb global warming by cutting greenhouse gas emissions . <p> The first part , labelled a " code red for humanity " when it was published last August , examined the physical basis of climate change , while the second @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rising temperatures and the options for -- and limits to -- adapting to them . <h> What is the IPCC ? <p> Advertisement <p> It is the UN body for assessing the science related to climate change . It was established in 1988 to provide political leaders with scientific assessments on climate change , to help them make policy . Some 195 countries are members of the IPCC . <p> The evidence is clear : the time for action is now . We can halve emissions by 2030 . <h> There always seems to be another climate report coming out . What is different about this one ? <p> The IPCC reports are an assessment of all the available science on climate change . <p> This latest study references more than 18,000 studies and sources , and has involved hundreds of authors from around the world , who have received tens of thousands of comments on earlier drafts from scientists and governments . <p> Most importantly , the 63-page summary of the report has been subject to a line-by-line approval process involving scientists and representatives of the 195 governments before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ two weeks . <p> It significantly overran its scheduled Friday finishing time , wrapping up the approval process late on Sunday evening , but it does mean that governments have signed off on the findings . <h> What does the report say ? <p> Advertisement <p> It warns that without deep and immediate cuts to greenhouse gas emissions in all sectors , it will be impossible to limit global warming to 1.5C , beyond which the most catastrophic impacts of climate change will be felt . <p> That means substantial reduction in the use of fossil fuels , a switch to clean energy such as increasingly cheap renewables , technology to capture carbon and scaling up everything from electric cars to energy efficiency in buildings . <p> It also highlights ways to encourage people to make greener choices , such as plant-based diets or choosing to walk and cycle , and warns that measures to take carbon from the atmosphere , including tree planting and new technology , will also be needed . <p> ( PA Graphics ) <h> What impact will it have ? <p> The first part of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate summit in Glasgow , which aimed -- and just about managed -- to keep limiting global warming to 1.5C within reach . <p> The second part of the report landed just days after the world was plunged into a geopolitical crisis with Russia 's invasion of Ukraine . <p> The war , which worsened already soaring energy prices as western nations scrambled to shift away from Russian oil and gas , has put security of supplies and fuel costs at the top of the agenda for many countries including Ireland . <p> Advertisement <p> Those backing climate action have seized on the report 's findings on the need for deep cuts in fossil fuel use , saying it is another reason to wean countries off oil and gas , alongside energy security and curbing Russian aggression . <p> But with fossil fuel companies putting pressure on governments to increase exploration and production , and with consumers facing a cost-of-living crisis , there is a danger -- once again -- that decisive climate action falls victim to more immediate concerns for politicians .

More info <p> Mummies in Chile that are more than 7,000 years old are now decomposing due to climate change . <h> In a nutshell <p> Climate change is making northern Chile , usually a dry area , more rainy and humid . This change in climate is causing ancient mummies , thousands of years older than Egyptian mummies , to decompose as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oldest mummies in the world are now at risk due to climate change . <p> The mummies of the Chinchoor culture in northern Chile date from between 5,000 BC and 500 BC . <p> The fishing community carefully prepared their dead by removing skin and organs and filling out the bodies with animal skins or clay and reeds . Some had their original skin put back onto reshaped bodies . <p> The remains , which were only found in the 20th century , include adults , children and babies . There were adorned with wigs of human hair , feathers and clay masks . <p> Bodies found in the Atacama Desert are 2,000 years older than the Egyptian mummies . <p> Mummies of the Chinchorro culture are exhibited at the San Miguel de Azapa archaeological museum in Camarones , Arica , Chile , on March 24 , 2022 . The mummies , which were found in the north of Chile at the start of the 20th century , are more than 7,000 years old , meaning they pre-date the Egyptian mummies by two millennia . ( MARTIN BERNETTI/AFP via Getty @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mummies have survived so well in part because the desert is a dry , arid place , however now more precipitation and moisture is in the area . <p> Many of the mummies have been moved to museums but some remain in the desert . Some were deliberately reburied by archaeologists . <p> It is n't known how many mummies there are in the desert . <p> Scientists have begun to notice that the deterioration of these bodies are accelerating . <p> In a 2015 report from the Harvard School of Engineering and Applied Sciences , scientists confirmed that rising humidity in the desert was making the mummies less likely to stay well preserved . <p> Gizmodo reports that in the same year , ( 2015 ) , it rained more than two inches in the Atacama Desert ; the equivalent of several years ' worth of rain , according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration . <h> Bigger picture <p> Climate change is a threat to archaeological sites around the world . <p> In Britain , sites dating back to Roman rule of the county are in danger @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to the elements . <p> A 2019 study found that some 3,000 prehistoric shell midden sites on the Farasan Islands in the Red Sea ( off the Saudi Arabian coast ) are at risk due to rising sea levels .

The reality is weapons , no matter how sophisticated , kill extensively and often indiscriminately . @@64046878 <p> THE WORLD HAS the tools and knowledge to combat the climate crisis -- it just needs to turn them into action , with less than three years to halt the rise carbon emissions . <p> That 's the message from an international report released today by the United Nations on climate change and how to stop it to save the planet . <p> Analysing scientific research from around the world , the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) has found greenhouse gas emissions can be reduced by 40% to 70% by 2050 if the right policies , infrastructure and technology are in place . <p> The report outlines that removing fossil fuel subsidies would reduce emissions and benefit the environment and sustainable development . <p> It lists several mitigation options that it describes as " technically viable " , " increasingly cost-effective " and " generally supported by the public " . <p> Those are : <p> solar energy <p> wind energy <p> electrification of urban systems <p> urban green infrastructure <p> energy efficiency <p> demand-side management <p> improved forest and crop/grassland management <p> reduced food waste and loss . <p> However , almost all mitigation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them to be implemented at a large scale . <p> The feasibility of different options varies depending on the time and place -- for instance , the use of geothermal energy is more suited to some areas than others . <p> Today 's report is the third and final part of the IPCC 's sixth assessment report ( AR6 ) , which has compiled and assessed the newest climate science to give policymakers an up-to-date picture of the current state of the climate . <p> In a statement , IPCC chair Dr Hoesung Lee said : " We are at a crossroads . The decisions we make now can secure a liveable future . " <p> " We have the tools and know-how required to limit warming , " he said . <p> " I am encouraged by climate action being taken in many countries . There are policies , regulations and market instruments that are proving effective . <p> " If these are scaled up and applied more widely and equitably , they can support deep emissions reductions and stimulate innovation . " <p> Advertisement <p> How the report @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hundreds of scientists and then approved by governments around the world . <p> Over the last two weeks , the details of the report 's summary for policymakers were finalised over video conferences before its release , alongside the full report , to the public later today . <p> The process , which was due to end on Friday , stretched into the weekend and only reached a conclusion late yesterday evening . And the results will be catastrophic . " <p> The report outlines that human-caused greenhouse gas emissions continued to rise during 2010 to 2019 and the average annual emissions during those years were higher than in any previous decade . <p> However , the rate of growth was lower than it was between 2000 and 2009 . <p> It notes that since the last cycle of IPCC reports in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ consistently expanded , avoiding emissions that would otherwise have been produced . <p> Paris Climate Agreement <p> But progress on finance for meeting the goals of the Paris Agreement -- that is , to keep global temperature rise well below 2 degrees Celsius and to try to limit it to 1.5 -- is still slow . <p> Every country that signed up to the Paris Agreement , including Ireland , was required to adopt a Nationally Determined Contribution -- a plan to cut emissions in line with the deal . <p> Today 's report says countries ' current NDCs make it likely that warming will exceed 1.5 degrees Celsius during the 21st century . Keeping it below 2 degrees will require a rapid acceleration of mitigation efforts after 2030 . <p> It 's expected that climate policies that were implemented by the end of 2020 will not reduce emissions to the level that was indicated in the NDCs . <p> To achieve that target , the report said carbon emissions need to drop 43% by 2030 and 84% by 2025 . <p> " It 's now or never , if @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said Jim Skea , a professor at Imperial College London and co-chair of the working group behind the report . <p> Without immediate and deep emissions reductions across all sectors , it will be impossible . <p> " Removing fossil fuel subsidies would reduce emissions , improve public revenue and macroeconomic performance , and yield other environmental and sustainable development benefits , " the summary for policymakers says . <p> " Subsidy removal may have adverse distributional impacts especially on the most economically vulnerable groups which , in some cases can be mitigated by measures such as redistributing revenue saved , all of which depend on national circumstances . <p> " Fossil fuel subsidy removal is projected by various studies to reduce global CO2 emissions by 1 -4% , and greenhouse gas emissions by up to 10% by 2030 , varying across regions . " <p> Climate activists have long called for ending fossil fuel subsidies , but some countries that benefit financially continually push back . <p> Drafts of an international climate deal at COP26 in November gradually weakened down a proposed commitment to accelerate the phasing out of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ urban areas can significantly reduce emissions by reducing or changing energy and material consumption ; electrification ; and enhancing carbon uptake and storage ( capturing emissions and preventing them from polluting the atmosphere ) . <p> In the transport sector , policies that firstly focus on demand for transport can reduce its emissions in developed countries and limit emissions growth in developing countries . <p> Electric vehicles powered by low-emissions electricity and sustainable biofuels will also be useful tools for reducing emissions in land-based transport . <p> Many mitigation measures in the transport would have additional benefits like better air quality , health , equitable access to transport services and reduced congestion . <p> It describes the use of carbon dioxide removal technology to counter emissions that will be difficult to target as " unavoidable " if net-zero emissions are to be achieved . <p> Importantly , it 's expected that the global economic benefit of limiting warming to 2 degrees Celsius would be higher than the cost of mitigation measures . <p> The costs of some renewable energy sources and batteries for electric vehicles have dropped while the use @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Limited access to economic , social and institutional resources can often mean people are more vulnerable to climate risks and less able to adapt to protect themselves , especially in developing countries . <p> The report says the Sustainable Development Goals ( SDGs ) adopted under the UN 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development can be used as a basis for evaluating climate action in the context of sustainable development . <p> International cooperation will be critical to achieving ambitious climate mitigation goals , the report outlines . <p> Cutting back on long-haul flights , switching to plant-based diets , climate-proofing buildings and other ways of cutting the consumption that drives energy demand could reduce greenhouse gas emissions 40-70% by 2050 . <p> Those with the most also pollute the most , the report said . More info <p> A 15-Minute City is a new concept that is getting lots of attention in the world of urban planning . <p> It is a climate friendly place where those who live in the area can find all their daily needs within a 15-minute radius of their homes . <p> So if you need to go to the pharmacy , you can get there by foot or if you want to exercise in a park , it 's also nearby . It 's a place where you do n't have to depend on driving around - preventing fossil fuels from entering our atmosphere . <p> And , if you 're not driving , you 're not spending your money on a car , tax , insurance , NCT @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wallet and even your physical and mental health if you 're walking around in the fresh air more often . <p> In fact , a similar programme in Oklahoma has reduced the city 's obesity problem . <p> Here is everything you need to know about this modern urban planning concept . <p> What Is a 15-Minute City ? <p> A 15-Minute City is defined as " an ideal geography where most human needs and many desires are located within a travel distance of 15 minutes " according to an article by urbanists Andres Duany and Robert Steuteville . <p> This means community amenities , facilities , and services should be 15-minutes from your home . <p> ( Image : Camilla Siggaard Andersen/Hassell/IIP ) <p> Generally , the amenities include : <p> Parks and playgrounds <p> Libraries <p> Schools <p> Grocery stores <p> Pharmacies <p> Hardware stores <p> Coffee shops <p> Medical and dental services <p> The broader idea is that when people live closer together and share more resources , they can avail of better access to services and social opportunities while the environment also benefits through more efficient @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plan to work , it 's also essential most journeys are taken by foot , bike and public transport to avoid congestion . <p> A 15-minute city promotes more walking , which may help reduce obesity rates and improve your mental health . <p> Greenhouse gas emission levels could also lower if there 's no need to hop in a car to avail of amenities and services . <p> The strategy creates more public spaces to socialise in . It allows local businesses to grow and it gives people places to spend more time with their friends and family . <p> If there 's more effort put into the area in terms of trees , vegetation and green space , it reduces flood risk and improves biodiversity , delivering further health and economic benefits for the area . <p> 15-Minute City <p> The Irish Institutional Property report also found Dublin has one of the longest daily commutes of all EU countries - one hour . Cutting down on this commute time by introducing office spaces in neighbourhoods could allow Dubliners more time of leisure and activities , as well as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ City ? <p> As of now , Dublin is not a 15-minute city even though it is among the most vulnerable places to things like rising sea levels , flooding and the impact of extreme heatwaves predicted if the planet warms past the threshold of 1.5 degrees . <p> But if the capital takes action and joins movements such as the C40 cities , they can combat climate change . <p> This can be done by creating streets that put people before cars and re-designing neighbourhoods around communities and citizens ' needs to give people cleaner air , more cost-efficient homes and food and waste policies that do n't harm the planet .

Simply put , they are lying . And the results will be catastrophic . C. Without immediate and deep emissions reductions across all sectors , it will be impossible . " <p> While there has been a stark warning given , they noted that there is increasing evidence of climate action . <p> IPCC Chairman Hoesung Lee said that while we are at a crossroads and the next steps are vital , we have the tools and know-how to make the change that is needed . <p> The report said that there are options for change in all sectors but in order to halve emissions by 2030 , there needs to be major transitions in the energy sector involving a substantial reduction in fossil fuel use , improved energy efficiency , and the use of alternative fuels . <p> Advertisement <p> While much of climate change is down to government policies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said individuals can also make changes . <p> Cutting down on long haul flights , going for plant based diets and climate-proofing buildings can all help reduce greenhouse gases . <p> " Rapid and deep changes in demand make it easier for every sector to reduce greenhouse gas emissions , " including construction , food consumption and transport , the report said . 
@@64049115 <h> Honey can flow in landscapes with dismal histories . But what futures are forecast in the honey 's waning from the wilds ? <p> Note : This is the second of a two-part publication about beekeeping , conflict , and the environment . The first part can be read here . <p> Since the 1990s Bosnian war ended , prickly native shrubbery and invasive species have rewilded former frontlines and the abandoned countryside . Inadvertently , the disaster has fostered nooks of biodiversity , flourishing in ecological niches and microclimates . <p> Underdevelopment also minimised land use and agrochemicals -- two major stressors on pollinators worldwide . On the nearly ideal forage fronts , however , local impacts of climate change are fast becoming apparent . Honey is getting ever harder to find . <p> " My wife and I are at war over bees , " Imam Hasim says . This soft-spoken beekeeper complained playfully to the anthropologist as his wife poured us delicate cups of Turkish coffee . " He squanders all the money on bees , " the wife finally says , her irritation giving way to a more playful tone . <p> Their house @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sapna , which fiercely resisted the Chetnik -- the Serb Republic army -- offensives in eastern Bosnia throughout the war . Imam Hasim led prayers in the trenches and tended to the funerals and other rites . His wife nursed the wounded and fed soldiers , though food was scarce . It seemed puzzling that this woman , known to be so generous , now objected to her husband 's devotion to apiculture . <p> ( Courtesy of : Larisa Jasarevic ) <p> Since the war , Hasim 's passion for bees has turned many Sapna residents into beekeepers . Early in May , they used to move their bees eastwards to the warmer banks of Drina River , on the border with the Serb Republic , the other entity of the Bosnian state . New forests of black locust bloomed there early , in pearl-white clusters . <p> There , curled florets safeguard nectar from spring showers and supply hives with surpluses for bees to store and beekeepers to harvest . Retreating to the Sapna hills , the local beekeepers would catch black locust opening later at the higher @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ intense , amber-hued honey , while meadows and a windfall of honeydew rounded up the season . <p> But since the last decade , the weather has become unbound from the seasons . Plants are weathering rapid-fire shifts in air and soil temperatures . Atypical precipitation causes droughts or floods while freeze-thaw cycles spiral into late spring . Beekeepers ' records show in detail how deeply the unseasonal weather affects bees and plant life . <p> ( Courtesy of : Larisa Jasarevic ) <p> Honeybees are often on the verge of starvation . Emergency feeding , hive medicine and maintenance are making small apiculture economically unsustainable . It is these rising costs that the imam 's wife was accounting for . <p> All the more reason to carry on with beekeeping , apiarists like Hasim think . " Bees are a gift from Allah , " the imam tells me in 2017 . " They are inspired by God and their inspiration should teach us lessons . But , sadly , what do we learn ? We are devastating the bees without realising that by ruining them we are ruining @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ role that bees play in pollinating 80 percent of flowering plants that make Earth marvellous and life-friendly . But the imam was probing deeper into the integral relationships between divinely inspired bees and our own species that constitute an Islamic meaning of being a human . " But what is a human , these days ? " Hasim wonders aloud , as the anthropologist makes pensive notes . <p> Hasim used the Arabic insan and left the question open and daunting . In doing so , he signalled the meaning of the human as a life-long becoming . Living a human life , in the Quranic sense the imam presumed , is living within the world of relationships : with the earth and heavens and between all the earthbound , nonhuman ummah -- communities like yourselves . <p> ( Courtesy of : Larisa Jasarevic ) <p> The relationships are material , reaping benefits for humans . However , because being human is a metaphysical process , these relationships presume responses and responsibilities . A human can approach them as gifts -- take and give back graciously -- or can slight @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do , in turn , makes and , potentially , undoes them . <p> To live by revelation , unswervingly , like a bee , is to be a gift : A mercy to the worlds . This species keeps the planet lush and joyous . The insect makes substances that are biologically inimitable , therapeutically promising , and thrilling in taste . <p> Too often we speak of animal instincts reductively , as " uncreative , " something done by default . On the second look , it is the modern human responses that can turn uninspired and uncreative . <p> The emergency food we give bees -- sugar and pollen substitutes -- are inadequate to ensure their health , immunity , or longevity . Worse still , the modern lifeways can not even ensure that bees and other species live in peace . <p> ( Courtesy of : Larisa Jasarevic ) <p> The havoc wreaked on the natural world is becoming loudly obvious and is just as rapidly covered up by the clamour of resourceful responses : risk planning , mitigation , or climate finance . These optimistic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Such resourcefulness , however , has urgent limits , because the planet is finite and the biosphere is undergoing irreversible damage . At the same time , the injury done to the inner human becoming is more subtle , easier to miss , and -- Islamic revelation teaches -- infinitely tragic . <p> In the present that has been rightly described as " catastrophic , " it is not just the lack of political will nor the intransigent carbon economies that are driving the Earth to ruin . At fault is also a poverty of late modern imagination . Local beekeeping points to an eco-practice grounded in metaphysical contemplations . <p> Honeybees are defined by honey : pure sweetness and inspiration . Humans , ultimately , become defined by their deeds . To taste the honey , properly , to savour its flavorful body along with the richness of its meanings , would itself be revelatory . What ominous notes could we taste in honey 's vanishing ? <p> Disclaimer : The viewpoints expressed by the authors do not necessarily reflect the opinions , viewpoints and editorial policies of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to TRT World Opinion -- please send them via email , to **30;380;TOOLONG <p> Larisa Jasarevic is an anthropologist . Her current research concerns the ways in which bees and beekeepers in Bosnia weather the changing climate . She is especially interested in the meaning of bees in Bosnian Muslim culture and the ecological insights of Islamic eschatology . Her research findings are the subject of the book ' Beekeeping in the End Times ' ( IUP 2023 ) and documentary ( in post-production ) . She is a fellow with the ISRF and a visiting scholar at the Max Planck Institute

@@64049288 <p> Can software help us prevent cancer , using data from millions of patients ? Do algorithms have a detrimental or beneficial impact on our environment ? After long discussions , the European Parliament is beginning to make a move on the Artificial Intelligence Act ( AI Act ) to address some of these questions . <p> We have now moved beyond the discussion on the importance of AI . This technology is n't something we consider for the future anymore but is instead a reality that has an impact on our everyday lives and in every economic sector . The list of revolutionary AI applications continues to grow at breakneck speed , from biotechnology and healthcare to climate change adaptation and smart farming , from sustainable transport to critical infrastructure . Yet , for decades , AI technology has been advancing at the speed of light while legislation has been static on the matter . That is , until now . <p> We must give AI the space it needs to develop to its full potential and to contribute to our societies <p> I am extremely lucky to have been appointed as the ENVI rapporteur of the AI @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the first to have an opinion on the matter . We are working hard to deliver a horizontal legislation that guarantees a high level of legal certainty , protects our societies and ensures respect for our European values . This is the only way for citizens and businesses to fully trust the technologies they use . <p> We must give AI the space it needs to develop to its full potential and to contribute to our societies . Let 's face the ugly truth : the EU is lagging behind in the world 's tech race . It 's fundamental that we smooth the way for SMEs and entrepreneurs to innovate , through regulatory sandboxes and a reduction of bureaucracy . <p> However , we can not give AI full rein . " Innovation through regulation " is the principle guiding me while drafting the ENVI opinion on the AI Act . <p> We have to establish common rules to provide AI with a cross-cutting approach . We must acknowledge the monumental opportunity it represents to all sectors in society . And by doing so , the EU has a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of AI worldwide , as it has already done with data protection through the GDPR . <p> We can become world leaders in niche sectors such as neurorights . While terms like " brain data " and " neurochips " may sound like something taken from a sci-fi movie , they are gradually becoming our reality . The tech giants have been announcing their developments in the field , such as the ability to decode thoughts in brain activity and process them in computers , or to monitor neuroactivity through brain microchips . We can not be caught off guard . EU legislation must get in the game and set the rules for all players . <p> But if we regard AI as an ally -- and not a foe -- where can it get us regarding the EU 's overall priorities ? The truth is that AI can be pivotal in the fight against climate change and in achieving the goals set by the Green Deal . In fact , it has the potential of reducing worldwide greenhouse emissions by four per cent by 2030 , according to PwC @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ally -- and not a foe -- where can it get us regarding the EU 's overall priorities ? The truth is that AI can be pivotal in the fight against climate change and in achieving the goals set by the Green Deal <p> It will lead the way in reaching climate neutrality through monitoring the environment , designing smarter and greener cities , reducing energy consumption , developing more efficient transportation and making our waste management more intelligent . Even though AI algorithms can have negative consequences for the environment through their energy consumption , they are still capable of reducing 10 times more greenhouse gas emissions than their own footprint . <p> I am determined to ensure that the deployment of AI only works for our benefit by demanding that the protection of the environment is considered a high-risk factor in our legislation . <p> Healthcare is another of the EU 's priorities that will be transformed by AI . We have an unprecedented opportunity to make use of emerging technologies to save millions of lives . From combating pandemics to enhancing research and diagnosis , from developing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' decision-making capabilities in matters of public health -- the benefits are countless . <p> However , wherever human lives are at stake there is a need to keep a sharp lookout . I am pushing for a deployment of AI under strict conditions to guarantee safety , prevent discrimination and biases , establish liability rules and ensure the protection of personal data . Legislation must lead innovation in the same way that AI will lead the future . But this future has to be designed only for the benefit of society .

@@64049406 <h> Small changes can have big cumulative effects on environment <p> In the second instalment of her series on climate change actions , Keir McNamara , acupuncturist , sports injury specialist and former agricultural scientist tells Bridget Ginnity that taking a small bit of personal responsibility can have a big effect cumulatively in the quest for sustainable living <p> THE past president of Uruguay , Jose Mujica , made an impression on me . He donated 90% of his salary to charity , lived on a bit of a farm outside the city instead of the presidential mansion , drove an old Volkswagen Beetle and said " I can live well with what I have . " <p> Travelling over the years , I saw how people in third world countries make do with modest amounts , which showed me that there is no need to have as much as we do . <p> I also witnessed at first-hand how human intervention has caused environmental disasters . <p> I spent time in Australia in the mid-nineties and saw how removing natural vegetation for agricultural land indirectly caused high levels of salinity in the soil . Previously good tillage land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 20 years ago , I was in California where a huge amount of irrigation is used and saw the unsustainability of that and the effects on water levels . <p> More recently , I 've been to New Zealand where they need a massive amount of irrigation for grassland growth to sustain their transition from sheep to dairy cows , again giving rise to water problems . <p> All of these disasters are caused by us taking too much from nature . <p> Farming in Ireland <p> In Ireland , we have n't had such large scale disasters and we 've seen a big turn back to sustainable agriculture and support of biodiversity in the last 10 years . <p> Nearly every farmer is happy to embrace this approach because they love the land and the wildlife around it . <p> Farmers are the people who spend most time in nature . We do a good job here and there is also a lot of positive energy being expended by Teagasc and the Department of Agriculture to look at ways that farmers can farm more sustainably . <p> I was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ agricultural science . After a few years working with Teagasc and in the meat industry , I decided to come back to Clare and go into the family business of acupuncture because I prefer the rural life . <p> I still do a bit of farming and have a few sheep -- I expect the neighbours would call it dog and stick farming , and it 's definitely hobby level . <p> I 've an ongoing interest in the agricultural world , also with regard to climate change and the environment . <p> There has been a lot of talk about limiting productivity in Ireland in order to reduce our greenhouse gas emissions . <p> It 's good that we are taking responsibility from a climate point of view but we should keep in mind that we produce food very sustainably in Ireland compared with other countries . <p> If we reduce exports , the market needs will be met by suppliers from other countries who farm in a less sustainable way than we do . <p> Also , from what I 've seen in other countries there can be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , which should be as important as the other issues . <p> I 'm also concerned that if we curtail production , we could lose expertise within a generation . <p> That would make it challenging if we had to ramp up quickly to meet supply needs due to the climate crisis or for other reasons . <p> Within a week of the start of the war in Ukraine , we 've seen how quickly problems with food supplies can happen . It 's back to an emergency World War II situation . <p> There is talk of dairy farmers moving to tillage to help compensate for the loss of production in Ukraine . I think that 's not likely to happen as most farmers have special expertise and are not the generalists that they were 70 years ago . <p> The machinery and manpower simply is n't there to change over on the huge scale that is being aspired to . <p> I try to keep an open mind about how we proceed and judge things on a case-by-case basis , and sometimes " needs must " . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ popular but genetic engineering may be able to help in dealing with the climate crisis . <p> Genetically modified animal feed has been developed that can reduce the methane emissions from cattle . Crops have been genetically modified to have greater pest resistance . <p> There 's a lot of research going on in this general area , including in Oakpark in Carlow . <p> If we are to produce the increased volume of food crops that may be needed , we may have to choose between permitting the use of genetically modified crops or the use of currently banned pesticides . We might end up reversing environmental gains that we have made in the past . <p> Individual actions <p> When my wife and I came back to settle in Clare , we built a new home and tried to build it as sustainably as we could afford . <p> It 's a passive house , and we put up a wind turbine and solar panels to help offset the impact we have in other activities we like to do . <p> At the time it was hard to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ aid . <p> People often ask me about the payback of the environmental measures I have put in but for me , there 's a big emotional payback . <p> On a windy day , the hot water is heated by the wind turbine , and on a sunny day it 's heated by the solar panels so I do n't feel one bit guilty having a long hot shower , all at zero carbon emissions . It 's not like when I was growing up and we had the big furore over who left the immersion switched on ! <p> After lockdown , we were very eager to get back to travel but there is a bit of emotional baggage in that regard . <p> There seems to be a big effort in the aviation world to reduce the adverse impact , and I 'm hoping that will let me continue to travel without feeling uneasy . <p> Improvements in fleet management can make air travel more efficient and there seems to be solid moves towards hybrid aircraft for the short haul flights . <p> I like to see @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ impact , like recycling and reducing food waste . <p> We do n't eat about 15-20% of the food we buy . Reducing that would make a huge difference . <p> Living in the countryside we 're very lucky that we have farm animals and pets to eat the leftovers and use up our food waste . <p> I 'd also encourage people to look at grants available for home upgrades . A lot of people would love to have solar panels or a wind turbine but for whatever reason , often cost , it 's not appropriate for them , but the small easy things can make a big difference . <p> I believe that a small bit of personal responsibility can have a big effect cumulatively . <p> Renewable energy / technology changes <p> I 'd like to see a future with more renewables and more embracing of technologies that are already there . <p> I ca n't emphasise enough how important it is that people have access to the democratic process and that their voice and concerns are heard as part of the planning process , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are running up against problems with planning . <p> While there are genuine individuals and groups , I 've heard that there is a different motivation by some people who are objecting . <p> The effort some people put in to objecting to planning applications is evidence of how strong their convictions are . <p> Valid concerns must be addressed and projects must progress in a reasonable timeframe to be commercially viable . <p> We 've seen recently that the company that were working with Moneypoint on their renewable projects in the estuary pulled away from it , due in part to how challenging they found the Irish system . <p> In Clare we 've had a big commitment to wind turbines and they 're not without their impact on the landscape and to individuals living near them , and have had lots of opposition . <p> There has to be storage capacity to sustain the renewable infrastructure that is planned , such as batteries or hydrogen generation yet we see very few of those being built . Legitimate concerns , such as lithium fires with batteries , stopped a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ big farming related issues is nutrient waste management . <p> Efforts at putting in water treatment plants and biodigesters have also been met with huge opposition . In other countries they seem to be able to get such projects done and still respond to the concerns of locals . <p> It would also be nice to see some joined-up thinking from government and local authorities . <p> New housing developments typically have great measures to make the individual house green but communal schemes like centralised heating systems or biodigester systems to supplement gas are n't included . It just does n't seem to be considered . <p> Communal schemes are not new or innovative and these technologies are already well established in other countries . <p> Small scale projects like energy from biodigestion or green waste incineration at local level do n't seem to be undertaken here and again you see them working successfully in places like Germany or Austria for instance . <p> Here we seem to go for projects that get too large and unsustainable and inevitably fail . One example was bringing woodchips from Clare and other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all with the best intentions . <p> Local actions <p> It was great during the lockdown that we all rediscovered our local area -- I found that I did n't have to drive to Lahinch for an enjoyable walk to get a sense of wellbeing . It 's lovely to enjoy what 's around you . <p> If you feel you have to travel all the time for leisure it becomes unsustainable in the long term . It was lovely to see the satisfaction people got during the pandemic from enjoying what was around them and engaging with that . <p> I 'm involved in the local Meitheal in Inch/Kilmaley/Connolly and we clean up along the roadsides through an An Taisce scheme . @@64049520 <p> Sara Baume is a woman of many talents . As well as being an acclaimed author , she continues to pursue a parallel career as a visual artist . Perhaps clairvoyance should also be added to the list of her accomplishments . <p> In her new novel , Seven Steeples , couple Bell and Sigh and their two dogs retreat to a rented farmhouse beside a mountain in West Cork where they cut themselves off from the outside world , immersing themselves in the mundane rituals of living . <p> It is a beautifully-written , hypnotic and thought-provoking book that echoes how during lockdown we retreated into our homes and ourselves . However , it turns out that the book was written when the pandemic and lockdown was , for the general population , an inconceivable prospect . <p> " I finished it in 2019 , more or less ... I did make changes and edits in the last two years but it was more or less a fait accompli by 2019 , " says Baume . " I started it before handiwork , my last non-fiction book , which is very much part of the same universe @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the coming pandemic . <p> " Seven Steeples ended up being very relevant . I think it 's funny how people will relate more than ever to this tiny domestic universe and trying to find some solace in doing the same thing at the same time at every day . " <p> Like Bell and Sigh , Baume and her partner Mark live in a rented farmhouse in West Cork with their two dogs . Seven Steeples also covers seven years in Bell and Sigh 's relationship -- Baume and her partner were in the seventh year of their relationship when she began to write the book . <p> " I started the book when I had been in a relationship for seven years , so I felt like I could talk about a seven-year relationship , I kind of knew what happened then . There was also this thing about the seven-year itch ... around that time , our friends were either breaking up with their partners or getting married . And then there is the other thing that the body renews itself every seven years , that every @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all those things . " <p> Baume ended up neither splitting up with her partner nor getting married . For me , it was darker than that , " says Baume . " It is going to be so hard to talk about the book without giving away the end , is it a good ending or a bad ending ? @@64050515 <h> We can stop climate change : IPCC report 's hopeful message <p> Thousands of Irish students took part in another climat protest today in Dublin and across the country to demand the Irish government an immediate action on climate change . On Friday , May 24 , 2019 , in Dublin , Ireland . ( Photo by Artur Widak/NurPhoto via Getty Images ) <p> Get the latest news from Buzz Climate straight to your inbox <p> Something went wrong , please try again later.Invalid Email <p> We use your sign-up to provide content in ways you 've consented to and to improve our understanding of you . This may include adverts from us and 3rd parties based on our understanding . You can unsubscribe at any time . More info <p> The latest report from the UN 's Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change is all about mitigation . There have been stark headlines covering the near-3000 page report but it does have a somewhat hopeful message -- there is still time to avoid the worst impacts of climate change . <h> Final warning <p> This latest report from the IPCC marks the third and final instalment of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was on the physical impacts of climate change . The second was on adaptation and this one is on mitigation , or how we can avoid its worst effects . <p> The verdict of this final instalment ? " It 's now or never " . <p> The mitigation report looked at what tools we have to stave off catastrophic consequences of climate change . These solutions include renewable energy , electric vehicles and low carbon technology . <p> It is also clear that fossil fuel usage needs to stop . In fact , fossil fuel infrastructure that 's already in place , under construction or planned will release far more carbon emissions into the air than the global carbon budget can allow . <p> A wind turbine on the Raheenleagh Wind Farm near Ballinvally , County Wicklow , Ireland , on Monday , Sept. 20 , 2021 . Ireland 's electricity grid warned of a potential capacity shortfall for the winter periods over the next five years . Photographer : Paulo Nunes dos Santos/Bloomberg via Getty Images <h> Renewable energy is ready <p> The report finds the costs of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ way cheaper than they used to be . <p> Renewable energy costs have taken a nosedive in the past decade and are now 85 per cent cheaper . This means the fossil fuel alternatives are not only plentiful , but cheap . <p> This means that our electricity grids and transportation can become a lot cleaner . <p> The cost of wind energy , for example , a very important source of energy for Ireland , has reduced by 55 per cent according to the IPCC between 2010 and 2019 . <p> The IPCC singles out wind and solar power as holding the greatest possible emissions savings by 2030 . Between them , they can save about 8 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent . <h> International action works <p> As you might expect from an UN-backed body , the IPCC is a fan of international cooperation . The report finds that at least 18 countries have sustained emissions cuts over the past decade . <p> Chair of the IPCC working group , Jim Skea , told The New Scientist that without laws and policies committing nations to emissions cuts @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than they are currently . <h> Carbon dioxide removal is scalable <p> Carbon capture technology is " unavoidable " if the world is to reach net zero greenhouse gas emission , the report says . <p> There are a few ways to do this . The natural way is to plant trees and rewet bogs . Then there are several technological solutions including bioenergy carbon capture and storage . This is where plants are grown , they capture carbon while they grow ( like all plants ) , then they are burned but the emissions they give off are captured and stored . The other technological solution is Direct Air Capture , this is less scalable . <p> The International Energy Agency ( IEA ) found that in 2020 , 31.5 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide were emitted . To put that in context , the world 's largest carbon capture plant can extract 4,000 tonnes of carbon dioxide per year -- and it only opened last September . <p> The IPCC report makes it clear that carbon capture will does not allow for continued fossil fuel use . <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a part to play in mitigating climate change . What the report refers to as " demand-side strategies " have an impact . This involves people changing their behaviour to be more environmentally conscious by buying second hand , using the car less and switching to a more plant-based diet . The IPCC estimates that actions such as these could collectively cut emissions by between 40 and 70 per cent by 2050 . <p> Individual action alone will not solve the climate crisis , but it can help . <h> We can afford it <p> The report is clear that mitigating for climate change is not too expensive and is worth doing . Every accounting for the economic costs brought on by climate change such as extreme weather events , the IPCC found that it will amount to just a few per cent of global GDP by 2050 . It will , however , require a massive effort from governments , businesses and individuals .

More info <h> In a nutshell <p> Yesterday , the D ? il approved Ireland 's first carbon budget . This will set a limit on greenhouse gas emissions the country can emit . <p> The D ? il did n't even vote on the budget , deeming it unnecessary due to the lack of opposition . Just six TDs , the six that make up the Rural Independents group , stood to oppose the motion . 10 is required to carry out a formal vote . <p> While TDs in favour of climate action raised concerns about weaknesses they identified in the budget , Rural Independents TDs said the budget would be detrimental to Irish farmers . <h> The first carbon budget <p> The first carbon budget will last until 2025 . It allows for a total of 295 million tonnes of emissions to be produced in the country . <p> Between 2026 and 2030 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of emissions and the following five year period will allow 151 million tonnes . <p> Now that the budgets have been passed by the D ? il , sectoral emissions targets will need to be agreed upon . <p> These emission ceilings will " determine how each sector of the economy will contribute to the achievement of the carbon budgets " , said junior minister Ossian Smyth , on proposing the motion on carbon budgets yesterday . <p> The sectoral limits should be presented to the government for approval by the end of June , Smyth said . <h> Draft targets <p> While the sectoral emission ceilings have n't been finalised , the Climate Action Plan published draft target ranges last November . <p> Compared to 2018 figure ; the Climate Action Plan suggested the following cuts between now and end of the decade . <p> Electricity : cut emissions between 62 per cent and 81 per cent <p> Buildings : cut emissions between 44 per cent and 56 per cent <p> Transport : cut emissions between 42 per cent and 50 per cent <p> Land and forestry : cut @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Industry : cut emissions between 29 per cent and 41 per cent <p> Agriculture : cut emissions between 22 per cent and 30 per cent <p> The report 's findings will be used to support climate change campaigns and awareness as well as influence national policy and climate action . ( Credit : Getty Images ) <h> Enough to tackle the climate crisis ? <p> During the D ? il debate , several TDs questioned if the budget would do enough for Ireland to fulfil its international obligations to cut emissions . <p> Newly appointed Labour leader Ivana Bacik pointed out " there is a degree of backloading " on the budgets - meaning we are doing less now with the promise of doing more in the future . <p> " I acknowledge this is to facilitate the adoption of new policies and practices but , again , we need to see a greater sense of urgency in these budgets , " Bacik said . <p> Backloading has already been acknowledged as an issue by the Oireachtas Committee on Climate Action who voted in favour of approving the proposed carbon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ review of backloading and for the government to ensure that the transition is just in all sectors of society . <p> " The IPCC was clear that there is a very small window of three years to meet targets . We know the effects of global warming are cumulative , so we will be worse off for not taking quick action now , " Bacik added . <p> Monday morning 's IPCC report gave the world just 30 months ( until 2025 ) to get off fossil fuels . <p> Ireland , like every other Western nation , is not prepared to do that . <p> People Before Profit/Solidarity TD Paul Murphy promised an amendment that would follow the IPCC 's latest report and force greater cuts in the carbon budgets . The amendment did not pass . It called on the environment minister Eamon Ryan to consult with the Climate Change Advisory Council again and amend the budgets in accordance with what they advise " to ensure the budget reflects our climate obligations based on the latest science and the principles of climate justice " . <p> Other TDs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up to the Programme for Government which promised an average of 7 per cent reduction in greenhouse gases every year . <p> " This is reiterated four times over two pages , " Jennifer Whitemore , Wicklow TD for the Social Democrats said . <p> The average reduction proposed by the carbon budgets is 5.7 per cent . <p> Green Party TD Brian Leddin , disputed this saying the 5.7 per cent figure is " an interpretation we do not agree with " . <p> Although the Irish Farmers Association previously told the Oireachtas that a 22 per cent emissions reduction target would be " challenging but achievable " , TDs of the Rural Independent Group argued the budget would hurt Irish farmers .

( 7/4 ) <p> It 'll see researchers from Atlantic Technology University collaborate with island communities and a range of tourism stakeholders , including Udaras na Gaeltachta , Failte Ireland , and local authorities . <p> The objective is to enable more reliable and accurate measuring and monitoring of key sustainable tourism indicators to facilitate evidence informed planning . <p> The project is adapted from the European Tourism Indicator System -- and criteria will include tourist spending patterns , inclusion and accessibility , transport impact , climate change , energy consumption , waste generation and sewage treatment . <p> The project is being launched at the ATU Connemara campus in Letterfrack this afternoon .

@@64050664 <p> The world has its best chance yet to reduce greenhouse gas emissions quickly , but hard and fast cuts are needed across all sectors and nations to hold warming to safe levels , the global authority on the climate crisis says . <p> The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) report , released today ( 4 April ) , says opportunities to affordably cut global emissions have risen sharply since the last assessment of this kind in 2014 . But the need to act has also become far more urgent . <p> The report is the definitive assessment of how well the world is doing in finding solutions to rising temperatures . We each contributed expertise to the report . Here , we explain key aspects of the findings and what it means for the world . <h> Earth remains on red alert <p> The report finds the world has made progress on emissions reduction over the last decade . Growth in greenhouse gas emissions slowed to 1.3pc per year in the 2010s , compared to 2.1pc in the 2000s . <p> But global emissions remain at record highs . If policy ambition does not ramp @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and be well on the way to 2 degrees Celsius -- failing to meet the temperature goals of the Paris Agreement . <p> Alarmingly , the world 's current policies put us on track for global warming of between 2.2 degrees Celsius and 3.5 degrees Celsius within 80 years . It 's far better than the 4 degrees Celsius or more feared about a decade ago , but still far from consistent with the Paris Agreement . <p> To have a 50pc chance of keeping global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius by century 's end , global CO2 emissions must halve in a decade , reach net zero in the 2050s and go net negative thereafter . <p> Methane emissions would also have to halve by 2050 in these scenarios . <p> Halving global emissions by 2030 is viable and achievable , the IPCC says . But it requires an immediate step change in climate policy across all sectors , countries and levels of government . Rich nations must make the most rapid emissions reductions . <h> More than technology <p> The report is a comprehensive catalogue of what can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- to avert devastating climate change . <p> Some trends are encouraging . Some 36 countries have successfully cut greenhouse gas emissions over more than a decade . And opportunities to cut emissions affordably and cheaply have multiplied enormously since 2014 , the report finds . <p> This is largely due to the plunging costs of renewables , which promises emissions reduction beyond the energy sector in areas such as manufacturing and heavy transport . <p> But change is not coming fast enough . The report confirms all energy efficiency gains in the last decade have been more than outpaced by economic and population growth . <p> Technology is not a silver bullet . To have a chance of halving global emissions by 2030 , we must use fewer high-carbon products and adopt less emissions-intensive lifestyles . Like all other changes required , these can not be incremental , the IPCC says . <h> No one gets left behind <p> In 2016 , the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals -- an action plan for people , planet and prosperity -- came into effect . <p> Sustainable development meets the needs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the latest IPCC report emphasises , it can not be achieved without effective climate action . <p> One Sustainable Development Goal explicitly focuses on tackling climate change . But climate action is linked to all other goals , including those relating to energy , cities , industry , land , water and people . <p> Emissions reduction policies must be inclusive and avoid unintended consequences such as exacerbating existing poverty and hunger . The transition to a low-carbon world should be equitable and leave no-one behind . <p> The IPCC report calls for both accelerated climate action and a just transition . This requires well-designed policies at all levels of government , and across all sectors . International cooperation is key . <h> Is the Paris Agreement working ? <p> This report is the first to assess the Paris Agreement , which took effect from 2020 . Under the agreement , countries submit and update pledges on emissions reduction and adapting to the changing climate . <p> For these pledges to be achieved globally , high-income countries must help other nations by providing finance , access to clean energy technologies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ identified a shortfall in global climate finance . In particular , high-income countries missed their 2020 target to mobilise $100bn per year . <p> The Paris Agreement is a treaty but the pledges are voluntary . Countries set their own targets and ca n't be forced to meet them . So , is it working ? <p> According to this new report , it largely is -- albeit slowly . For instance , it has encouraged nations such as Australia to make more ambitious emissions pledges . It has also enhanced transparency , enabling outside groups , such as those in civil society , to assess countries ' progress . <p> Other international mechanisms , such as global business partnerships and youth climate protests , are also driving change . But more must be done to halve emissions this decade . <h> Cities are central <p> The IPCC report found around 70pc of the world 's greenhouse gas emissions are produced in cities and urban areas . This offers both challenges and opportunities for emissions reduction . <p> To date , more than 1,000 cities worldwide have signed up to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more cities must step up and work towards goals such as 100pc renewable energy , zero-carbon transport , decarbonising construction and improving waste management . <p> Developing countries are rapidly urbanising , which requires new housing and infrastructure . But doing so in a business-as-usual way could lead to substantial new emissions , the IPCC warns . <p> City leaders must embrace integrated planning and management to meet the climate challenge . This must be achieved while cities continue their important roles in maintaining social , economic and environmental well-being .
